Section 6 
§ 105.4 (b) (7) 
Service restoration procedures. Provide the corporation’s procedures for 
mobilizing its personnel, materials and equipment in order to survey 
system damage and implement measures to ensure timely, efficient and 
safe restoration of service to customers in areas damaged by a storm or 
other storm-like electric emergency. The procedures need to identify 
restoration priorities to ensure that restoration time is minimized, while 
ensuring critical customers’ needs are met. Include a listing of the 
priorities for service restoration among customer groups in these 
procedures. Identify criteria for determining when centralized versus 
decentralized control is appropriate. For those severe emergencies when 
field damage assessments are needed, describe the methods for making, 
within 24 hours, broad scale preliminary assessments of the nature and 
extent of system damage based on rapid surveys of damaged areas and 
other data sources, and for making, within 48 hours, more detailed 
estimates of system damage based on systematic field surveys. Describe 
how field reports of system damage will be integrated with damage 
reports or indicators from other sources, such as customer call-ins, in 
order to make a reasonably accurate assessment of system damage and 
reliable projections of the personnel, equipment, materials and time that 
will be needed to rapidly and safely achieve service restoration goals in 
all damaged areas. Provide the procedures for deploying company and 
mutual aid crews to work assignment areas, monitoring crew activity, 
reassigning crews as necessary and releasing crews, under both 
centralized and decentralized command modes. Describe the methods 
and means that will be used to communicate with damage survey crews 
and service restoration crews. Identify the procedures for coordinating 
company restoration procedures with those of other utilities’ restoration 
efforts and with state and local emergency management and public 
works agency efforts.

§ 105.4 
(e) (2) 
Any electric corporation may request that the commission designate as

1. Introduction 
§ 105.5 
Commission review and approval. Upon receipt and review of emergency 
plans or amendments filed by an electric corporation under this Part, the 
commission may require any such corporation to modify such plans or 
amendments or otherwise prescribe conditions for approval. Approval 
will be based on compliance with the requirements of this Part. 
 
§ 105.6 
Compliance with electric emergency plans 
 
§105.6 (a) 
Each electric corporation shall comply with the guidelines and practices 
set forth in its effective emergency plans. Each electric corporation shall 
comply with any additional electric emergency plan requirements that 
may be imposed by the commission. 
 
§105.6 (b) 
Under emergency conditions, an electric corporation may modify its 
response from that in the filed electric emergency plan to the extent 
required to restore service in a safe and efficient manner. However, 
modifications and the circumstances that caused them shall be reported in 
writing to the secretary of the commission within 60 days from restoration 
of full service. Minor changes such as telephone numbers, personnel 
changes, etc., need not be reported, but as soon as practicable should be 
made to the plans.

 NYSEG and RG&E Electric Utility Emergency Plan Appendix B  
 
B-20 
 
 
 CASE 04-M-0159 APPENDIX B  
EVENT NOTIFICATION REQUIREMENTS  
ALL NOTIFICATIONS SHALL BE MADE WITHIN ONE HOUR OF AN 
INCIDENT OR EVENT UNLESS OTHERWISE SPECIFIED  
I. SYSTEM CONTROL - REPORTS OF IMPENDING EMERGENCIES, EMERGENCIES, AND LOAD 
CURTAILMENT  
 
A. Requests for curtailed electric use, voltage reductions, and load shedding 
initiated to maintain the adequacy of the electric system and significant bulk 
supply outages or accidents of consequence are to be reported to the Office of 
Electric, Gas and Water. The specific items to be brought to the Office’s attention 
are as follows:  
 
1. Any decision to issue a request for customer reduction in use of electricity. 
The Office of Electric, Gas and Water is to be notified at the time of 
decision to issue any such request.  
 
2. Any action to maintain the adequacy of the bulk electric system by 
reducing firm customer loads by voltage reductions, manual switching, 
operation of automatic load shedding devices, or any other means. The 
Office of Electric, Gas and Water is to be notified at the time of decision to 
take such action.  
 
3. Any bulk supply outage that has, or could have, a significant impact on the 
utility’s electric system or the state-wide system.  
 
B. The following information is to be included in the reports:  
 
1. 1. For Items I.A.1. and I.A.2., the utility shall provide the approximate 
area(s) affected the time of the action, the time and/or an estimate of the 
time of restoration of normal service (or cancellation of a customer 
request), an estimate of the amount of load reduction expected or load 
interrupted, and the number of customers affected if load is interrupted.  
 
Exhibit __ (EPP-2) 
Page 149 of 325


--- Page 153 ---
 
 NYSEG and RG&E Electric Utility Emergency Plan Appendix B  
 
B-21 
 
2. 2. For Item I.A.3., the utility shall provide a description of the incident and 
events leading to its occurrence, the time of occurrence, the system(s) 
affected, and an evaluation of the effect on the system(s).  
 
II. LOSS OF ELECTRIC SERVICE  
A. Written reports of electric service interruptions of five minutes or more are 
required by 16 NYCRR Part 97. Such reports are to be prepared in accordance 
with the regulations and submitted to the Office of Electric, Gas and Water.  
 
B. Additionally, notice is to be made for each of the following events:  
 
1. Loss of electric service to 5,000 customers or more lasting 30 minutes or 
more;  
 
2. Any loss of a distribution system network;  
3. Loss of service resulting from load shedding; and  
4. Implementation of voltage reduction  
 
C. Notice of these events requires telephonic notification during business hours. 
Events occurring after business hours shall be reported as prescribed by the 
Director of the Office of Electric, Gas and Water  
 
D. The following information should be provided in the notice:  
 
1. The approximate territory affected.  
 
2. The date and time of the incident causing the interruption.  
 
3. The expected duration of the interruption.  
 
4. If restored at the time of the call, the date and time of restoration.  
 
5. The number of customers affected and amount of load involved.  
Exhibit __ (EPP-2) 
Page 150 of 325


--- Page 154 ---
 
 NYSEG and RG&E Electric Utility Emergency Plan Appendix B  
 
B-22 
 
 
6. A listing of any critical services affected.  
 
7. A description of the incident and its cause.  
8. Any follow-up actions planned  
 
III. REPORTS OF PERSONAL INJURY ACCIDENTS  
A. Notification of electric system personal injury accidents and deaths are required 
by 16 NYCRR Part 125. This requirement applies to all electric system accidents 
that result in injury or death to a non-employee and/or inpatient hospitalization or 
death to an employee or contractor employed by the utility, including accidents 
that occur at generating plants.  
 
B. All reports are to be made in accordance with the regulations and the following 
requirements and submitted to the Office of Electric, Gas and Water.  
 
1. Reports for accidents, except those involving a fatality or major media 
attention, occurring after business hours shall be made no later than 8:30 
a.m. of the next business day.  
 
2. Written reports shall be made using the Department’s standard form and 
may be submitted via e-mail or fax.  
 
3. Reports should include the following information:  
 
a. The location of the accident.  
 
b. The date and time of the accident.  
 
c. Whether or not the injured party is a utility employee or contractor.  
 
d. A description of the injuries sustained and the status of the injured party.  
 
e. A description of the accident and its cause.  
 
f. The time the utility received notification of the incident.  
 
Exhibit __ (EPP-2) 
Page 151 of 325


--- Page 155 ---
 
 NYSEG and RG&E Electric Utility Emergency Plan Appendix B  
 
B-23 
 
 
g. The time the first utility personnel arrived at the scene.  
 
h. The time qualified utility personnel arrived at the scene (i.e. personnel 
capable of addressing any safety hazard).  
i. Whether response operations were affected until utility personnel 
arrived.  
 
.  
IV. REPORT OF SHOCK INCIDENTS AND MOTOR VEHICLE ACCIDENTS  
A. All electric shock incidents shall be reported via the EIRS system.  
 
B. Electric shock incidents involving animals shall be reported.  
 
C. Motor vehicle accidents involving utility facilities in which there is a fatality, 
personal injury, or delay in rescue operations shall be reported.  
 
D. Reports for incidents occurring after business hours shall be made no later than 
8:30 a.m. of the next business day.  
 
E. The reports should include the following information:  
 
1. The location of the incident.  
 
2. The date and time of the incident.  
 
3. Whether or not the party who was shocked or injured, as appropriate, is a 
utility employee or contractor.  
 
4. A description of the condition of the affected party, and, as appropriate, of 
the injuries sustained.  
 
5. A description of the incident and its cause.  
 
6. The time the utility received notification of the incident.  
 
7. The time the first utility personnel arrived at the scene.  
 
8. The time qualified utility personnel arrived at the scene (i.e., personnel 
capable of addressing any safety hazard).  
Exhibit __ (EPP-2) 
Page 152 of 325


--- Page 156 ---
 
 NYSEG and RG&E Electric Utility Emergency Plan Appendix B  
 
B-24 
 
9. Whether response operations were affected until utility personnel arrived.  
 
V. MAJOR EVENTS  
Immediate telephonic notification regardless of the time of day is to be made for 
major events associated with a utility’s electric system that will likely result in 
widespread media attention. Examples of major events include, but are not limited 
to, load shedding, catastrophic storm emergencies, floods, fires, or nuclear 
radiation releases.  
Notification via the EIRS system is also to be made whenever a utility’s corporate 
emergency command center (e.g., storm center) becomes operational.  
 
VI. MANNER OF NOTIFICATION  
Except where otherwise noted above, the Director of the Office of Electric, Gas 
and Water shall prescribe the manner in which notice to Staff is to be provided.

 NYSEG and RG&E Electric Utility Emergency Plan Appendix C  
 
C-55 
 
DEPARTMENT OF HOMELAND SECURITY 
MUTUAL ASSISTANCE CANADIAN BORDER CROSSING LETTER 
 
 
Date: 
 
Nikhil Natarajan, Deputy Director 
New York State Office of Emergency Management 
1220 Washington Avenue 
Building 22, Suite 101 
Albany, NY 12226-2251 
 
Dear Deputy Director Natarajan, 
AVANAGRID NY] is currently preparing for the [Type of event] expected to begin, [Month Day, 202X].  As 
part of our preparation efforts to ensure we are able to quickly address anticipated power outages, we are 
seeking the assistance of electrical workers from Canada.  [Contractor] is crossing at the [Point of Entry] 
on the [State] border and will be working in [Utility]’s [Identify service location or divisions].  We have 
determined that the nearest available US electrical workers reside in states further away and/or are not 
readily available to support the power restoration effort.   
AVANAGRID NY requests the New York Emergency Management Agency to contact US Customs and 
Border Protection and request they allow entry of these Canadian electrical workers into the US for the 
mutual assistance support of [Utility] in New York.  Attach is the CBP Pre-Arrival spreadsheet.  It is 
expected that the electrical workers will be crossing the border between [Time and Time] on [Month Day, 
202X].  It is also our expectation that the Canadian electrical workers will assist in storm restoration 
efforts, ending their assignment by [Month Day, 202X], or earlier  
Sincerely,   
 
[Name and title of signatory] 
CC: 
Kevin Wisely New York State Department of Public Service 
 
Christian Bonvin New York State Department of Public Service 
 
[Point Of Entry]

UGEP – Part A

Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
Guiding Principles 
 
Safe, reliable gas service is vital to the welfare and comfort of Avangrid Networks’ Gas 
Operations Companies (collectively referred to as the “Companies”) customers. The Companies 
consider providing customers with reliable service to be a responsibility of the highest order. The 
procedures and policies described in the Unified Gas Emergency Plan (Plan) illustrate the 
Companies' readiness to handle emergencies. When emergencies interrupt service to 
customers, employees are mobilized to enable a safe, organized, and efficient response. 
For all classes of emergency, the main objective of the Companies’ Plan is to ensure the safe, 
fast, and reliable restoration of gas service to the customers in our franchises with the priority on 
safety to the public and then property. While this Plan emphasizes an all-hazards response and 
the roles played by Corporate and Division/Regional personnel during major emergencies, the 
Plan also supports day-to-day minor emergencies.  By coordinating communications and repair 
efforts, the Companies can monitor and address customers' concerns effectively. 
The Companies are committed to having a trained workforce available at all times to implement 
necessary emergency procedures. Given that restoration work can be dangerous, it is critical 
that everyone continue to put safety first and to look out for one another during these challenging 
events. 
Each emergency, by its very nature, is unique and offers opportunities to learn from the 
experience. We will continue to evaluate our response to each emergency and to amend or 
modify these procedures, as appropriate. Our goal will continue to be safe and efficient 
restoration of service to our customers during these challenging events.

Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
1 Overview 
 
1.1 Introduction 
This document details emergency plans in place at Avangrid Networks’ Gas Operating 
Companies (collectively referred to as the “Companies”).  The Gas Emergency Plan (Plan) is 
intended to ensure adequate response for all emergencies.  However, the principles of the 
Incident Command System (ICS), as incorporated into this Plan, apply to all types of unplanned 
events and will be used accordingly.   
The objectives of the Plan are to: 
• 
Provide prompt and efficient response to emergencies to facilitate maximum public 
safety. 
• 
Establish procedures that facilitate prompt and efficient emergency response utilizing the 
Incident Command System (ICS) concepts; 
• 
Be applicable to a variety of unplanned emergency situations which may occur, including 
but not limited to: 
o Natural disasters such as flooding, wildfires or earthquakes. 
o Emergency shutdown or pressure reduction in part of the gas system to protect 
life or property. 
o Gas supply curtailments, interruptions or pressure fluctuations. 
o Equipment or component failure. 
o Fire or explosion. 
• 
Minimize service interruption time and the resulting impacts to customers; 
• 
Provide information to customers and officials on restoration and overall emergency 
response; and  
• 
Provide a measure for evaluating the Companies' recovery from emergencies. 
• 
Outline the training available to emergency response personnel 
 
The Plan is a framework for responding to events that are, or could be, impacting the 
Companies’ Gas pipelines and facilities in the states of Connecticut, Maine, Massachusetts or 
New York. This document is intended to provide the user with information necessary to 
understand how the Companies identifies, classifies and responds to gas1 events and 
emergencies.  
While this document incorporates event-specific information for events reasonably predicted to 
occur on a pipeline system, this document cannot be considered comprehensive; the complexity 
and scale of a gas transmission and distribution system, the environment in which it operates, 
and the actions of people necessitate a flexible framework. The framework described within is 
intended to allow the Companies to efficiently and effectively identify the level of response 
necessary to respond to an unplanned event with the limited information typically available at 
the outset. The Incident Command System (ICS) concepts will allow the Companies to identify 
the tactics, strategies, resources and timelines necessary to make safe, assess, and restore the 
system. 
 
1 This document does not govern the Companies’ electric operations. 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 7 of 119


--- Page 336 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
8 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
 
1.2 Safety 
An emergency is a situation or occurrence of a serious nature, developing suddenly and/or 
unexpectedly, and demanding immediate action to minimize potential hazards to people and/or 
property. 
This emergency plan is designed to facilitate quick and proper reaction to emergency situations, 
with a priority toward protecting human life first and then property. First, the Companies shall 
make safe any hazardous condition related to the gas system and coordinate activities with 
public safety organizations. Once system integrity has been confirmed and the system has been 
returned to normal operations, customer service will be re-established or confirmed. 
Specific restoration priorities and procedures may be maintained by each Company in 
accordance with any state-specific requirements and Company policies. 
At all times, the Companies’ personnel and mutual assistance personnel will place safety first 
for both the public and themselves. 
1.3 Definitions 
Abnormal event: An unexpected, generally undesirable, condition affecting the gas system. 
Activated: Resources have been engaged in the response to the event or emergency. 
Awareness: Personnel have been informed of a situation, but no direct action is expected of 
them until further notice. 
Command Staff: The staff who report directly to the Incident Commander, including the Public 
Information Officer, Safety Officer, Liaison Officer, and other positions as required. 
Emergency2: An occurrence affecting the gas system, outside the typical course of business 
and with the potential for harm to personnel or significant property damage, requiring a 
significant response from Company personnel and resources. Emergencies often disrupt normal 
operations in the geography in which they are occurring. 
Emergency Operations Center (EOC): A location in which emergency management personnel 
are assembled, and from which an emergency is coordinated. 
Event: An occurrence affecting the gas system, outside the typical course of business, requiring 
a response from Company personnel and resources. 
General Staff:  A group of incident personnel organized according to function and reporting to 
the Incident Commander or Unified Command. The ICS General Staff consists of the 
Operations Section Chief, Planning Section Chief, Logistics Section Chief, 
Finance/Administration Section Chief. 
Isolated: Limited to a single geographic area or requiring limited resources for resolution. 
Major media: International, national or metropolitan news organizations. 
 
2 Specific states may define “emergency” in a more limited manner. See the relevant Part B for additional 
definition. 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 8 of 119


--- Page 337 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
9 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
Personnel: Company employees, and contractors. 
Standby: Resources have been alerted to a possible activation and are in a state of readiness. 
 
2 Document Information 
 
2.1 Plan Organization 
The Plan is organized in two components, Part A and Part B.  
Part A (this document) is applicable to all Avangrid Networks’ Gas Operating Companies;  
• 
The Berkshire Gas Company (BGC) 
• 
Connecticut Natural Gas Corp. (CNG) 
• 
Maine Natural Gas Corp. (MNG) 
• 
New York State Electric & Gas Corp. (NYSEG) 
• 
Rochester Gas & Electric Corp. (RG&E) 
• 
The Southern Connecticut Gas Company (SCG) 
 
Part B is specific to the Companies operating in a specific state; Connecticut, Maine, 
Massachusetts or New York. 
For any Company, both Part A and the applicable Part B document comprise a complete 
emergency plan. Additional materials supporting these plans are maintained in the Gas 
Emergency SharePoint intranet site (See Section 2.3). 
 
2.2 Plan Use 
Each Plan component contains both procedural and reference material intended for use during 
training and preparation for, and response to, gas system events and emergencies. It is 
imperative that personnel utilize the corresponding Part B document corresponding to the state 
in which their Company operates. 
Select topics appear in both Part A, and Part B, often with more specific company or state 
processes and requirements in Part B. Follow references within each Part as applicable.  
Additional emergency response reference material is also available to personnel through the 
Companies’ internal document sharing network, referred to as the “Gas Emergency SharePoint 
Site”. 
In all cases, work performed on the gas system will be performed in accordance with Company 
policies, standards, and O&M procedures, utilizing qualified personnel and calibrated equipment 
as governed by those documents.

2.5 Intended Audience 
The primary audiences for the Plan are personnel who may be coordinating or responding to 
events and emergencies on the Companies’ gas systems. The information contained herein has 
been selected and formatted to optimize use during an event or emergency.  
This document is not intended to serve as a comprehensive volume of all relevant information, 
only that which is applicable to the safe, effective response to an event or emergency on the 
gas system. 
2.6 Distribution and Availability 
This document is made available to all Company personnel and departments assigned a role in 
the Companies’ gas emergency response processes, and appropriate state and municipal 
agencies as necessary. 
The current revision of the Plan is available on the gas business page of the Companies’ Gas 
Emergency SharePoint Site.  
A copy of the most current Plan should be available in each local gas operations office. The 
local copies may be maintained either as a printed document, or digital document that is not 
stored on a corporate network such that they are available during a network outage. 
 
2.7 Questions and Continuous Improvement 
For questions during an emergency, personnel should contact their direct supervisor, section 
leader, or the Incident Commander. 
For non-emergency questions and continuous improvement opportunities, please contact Gas 
Engineering Technical Services.

3 Determination of an Event or Emergency 
 
3.1 Observation of Event or Emergency 
All personnel may identify a potential event or emergency. 
If personnel suspect that an observation or event may constitute a threat to the gas system, 
they should contact their supervisor and the Gas Control Center immediately. 
Contractors should contact emergency services (911) and their Company liaison. 
When communicating with supervisors or the Gas Control Center, personnel should provide as 
much information as is available. Such information may include: 
• 
Physical location (e.g. street address and cross street, highway and mile marker, 
landmark proximity) 
• 
Gas system information (e.g. system designation, facility location, pressure 
class) 
• 
Threat information (e.g. weather, flooding, vehicular impact, fire, civil 
disturbance) 
• 
Time of initial event or observation 
• 
Conditions that have changed during your observation (e.g. water level rising, fire 
spreading, service interruptions increasing in number) 
3.1.1 Receiving and Handling of Customer Calls  
Public observations will likely be received by Avangrid’s customer call centers by region. The 
call centers can receive and process multiple incoming calls simultaneously, routing emergency 
calls to Gas Dispatch for immediate response. 
The call centers utilize standard operating procedures and scripts for the handling of natural gas 
and carbon monoxide-related calls within, or near the Companies’ franchise areas. 
Emergency Services observations will likely be received directly by Gas Dispatch. Gas Dispatch 
utilizes procedures for the receipt of, and response to, customer emergency calls. 
Figure 3.1.1 below depicts the sources and flow of information during a reported emergency.

3.2 Declaration of Event or Emergency 
The Gas Control Center or Gas Dispatch will contact the appropriate operations leader or 
Incident Commander and Emergency Planning based on the geography of the event or 
emergency.  
The Incident Commander, in consultation with internal leadership, shall determine the 
appropriate event or emergency classification level as depicted in Table 3.1 below. 
Directors and officers of Gas Operations may declare event and emergencies and determine 
classification level and response. 
 
3.3 Determination of Classification Level 
The Companies have adopted a classification system to aid in determining and communicating 
the appropriate response to events and emergencies. 
The appropriate level corresponds to the level of ICS deployment appropriate to effectively and 
efficiently command and coordinate the situation; Table 3.1 below is intended to aid the 
authorized person(s) in the determination of the most appropriate event/emergency level.

3.3.1 Process of Event Classification 
To use the classification matrix, the user should first review the “Criteria” and “Potential Impacts” 
column to identify any key attributes of the situation that may match. Both columns are 
suggestive in nature; the user may find that the listed attributes do not fit the situation exactly; 
the user should identify the row that most closely aligns with the situation. 
Once the classification is chosen, the “Response” column will suggest the appropriate 
deployment of ICS resources. 
The “Notifications” column is suggestive and not prescriptive; refer to Part B: “Notifications” 
section of the Plan.

authorized persons to direct tactics and resources as appropriate. 
Note: For purposes of event/emergency classification, possible criteria for any given level are 
inclusive of those listed in the emergency level(s) below.

3.5 Gas Event Levels 
 
A Level 1 event is “worst-case-scenario” that necessitates the engagement of mutual aid 
assistance due to the exhaustion of the Companies’ resources. Routine business is 
generally halted during a Level 1 event. 
Typical Examples 
Such events may include, but are not limited to: 
 
-Devastating acts of nature (e.g. hurricane, flooding) 
 
-Multiple state outage affecting thousands of customers  
 
-Massive gas system disruption requiring extensive operations and relighting 
Typical Response 
Area Command and ICS organizations are activated and fully staffed. Communications are 
coordinated through Corporate Communications team. Dedicated liaison to governmental 
agencies may be required. 
Avangrid Unified Command may be activated if the emergency affects both gas and electric 
assets. 
Mutual aid resources have been requested. 
Level 1: Major Emergency 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 15 of 119


--- Page 344 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
16 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
 
Level 2: Avangrid Networks (or multiple-Company) 
Emergency 
A Level 2 event is that which involves a broad swath of the Companies’ gas systems 
requiring a company-wide response. Level 2 events are similar to Level 1 events with the 
exception that mutual aid may not be needed. Typically, routine business is paused while 
the emergency is addressed. 
Typical Examples 
Such events may include, but are not limited to: 
 
-Devastating acts of nature (e.g. hurricane, flooding) 
 
-Multiple state outage affecting thousands of customers  
 
-Massive gas system disruption requiring extensive operations and relighting 
Typical Response 
Area Command and ICS organizations are activated and fully staffed. Communications are 
coordinated through the Corporate Communications team. Dedicated liaison to 
governmental agencies may be required. 
Avangrid Unified command may be activated if the emergency affects both gas and electric 
assets. 
Mutual aid partners have been made aware that need may arise.

Level 3: Company Event 
A Level 3 event is that which affects a single Company and is that which involves more 
customers or assets than can be managed by Division resources. This classification should 
be considered if the event includes loss of life or hospitalization regardless of event scale. 
An event at this classification is likely to present a significant operational impact to an 
Operation Company. 
Typical Examples 
Such events may include, but are not limited to: 
 
-Gas-involved loss of life or hospitalization of employee, contractor or customer 
 
-Large scale outage affecting large number of customers 
 
-Outages or events requiring large-scale testing of assets or relighting of customers 
Typical Response 
ICS fully activated with extensive staffing. Area Command staffing on standby for activation 
should conditions warrant.  
All sections of the ICS structure are occupied by dedicated resources. Communications are 
coordinated through Corporate Communications team. 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 17 of 119


--- Page 346 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
18 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
 
 
Level 4: Division Event 
A Level 4 event is that which involves more customers or assets than can be managed by 
local resources alone. This classification should be considered if the number of affected 
customers or assets exceed local resources either due to volume or geographical extent of 
impact. 
Typical Examples 
Such events may include, but are not limited to: 
 
-Unexpected change in flow or pressure on a distribution system 
 
-Interruption of supply to a distribution system 
 
-Gas outage affecting a larger number of customers 
-Planned deployment of resources to many customers (e.g. shut-offs ahead of 
expected flooding, relight of a large affected area) 
-Events requiring coordination with multiple first-response entities1 (regardless of 
gas asset impact).  
-Events with two or more sets of local resources deployed 
Typical Response 
Select ICS personnel activated in the region. Local resources organized into strike teams 
under the Operations Section. Additional ICS resources placed on standby for activation 
should conditions warrant. 
There may be limited logistics, planning, finance or external communications activities at a 
Level 4; duties may be borne by personnel in the General Staff or Operations Section.

Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
 
 
3.6 Escalation/De-escalation 
Events and emergencies tend to evolve over time, with changes in scope, resources and 
tactics. Incident Commanders may use their judgement, in consultation with leadership and key 
stakeholders, to escalate or de-escalate the event or emergency classification level based on 
current conditions. 
Changes in classifications are at the discretion of the Incident Commander and do not need to 
be sequential. 
The intent of the classification level is to broadcast to event stakeholders the severity and level 
of response needed at that time. As the event develops, responding ICS resources may be 
activated or deactivated based on need. 
 
3.7 Cessation of Event or Emergency 
For Level 4 through Level 1 events or emergencies, criteria will be developed in the operational 
cycle to trigger a return to ‘normal operations’ and deactivate ICS resources. Various resources 
may remain engaged in activities driven by the event or emergency, but a formal cessation of 
the event or emergency will result in the de-activation of the ICS organization. 
For Level 5 events, cessation may be informal and is at the discretion of the incident leader.  
Level 5: Local Event 
A Level 5 event is that which involves few customers or assets and can likely be resolved 
by local resources. 
Typical Examples 
Such events may include, but are not limited to: 
 
-Vehicular impact to distribution assets 
 
-Third-party or outside force damage to distribution assets 
 
-Isolated, unplanned release of natural gas 
 
-Localized gas outage 
-Other low-impact events not requiring emergency responders (e.g. fire, police, 
EMT/EMS, ambulance personnel) 
Typical Response 
Local operations and management resources operating under normal chain of command.  
Typically, the ICS structure is not deployed during an event of this nature due to the 
capacity of local resources available to respond. If additional resources or coordination is 
required, the authorized person should consider whether an escalation to a more serious 
classification is warranted.

UGEP – Part A

Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
 
4 Notifications 
 
Notifications to public safety organizations shall be made as appropriate. 
Federal and state regulatory agencies and other critical government organizations shall be 
notified in accordance with the Companies’ standards and procedures.  
The Companies shall comply with all standards and regulations relevant to gas emergency 
operations set by the U.S. Department of Transportation and state regulatory agencies. 
4.1 Federal 
Reportable events will also require the preparation and filing of an incident report as soon as 
practicable, but no later than 30 days after discovery of the event. 
4.1.1 Federal Reporting Criteria 
The reportable incident definitions set forth by PHMSA in 49 CFR 191 are as follows: 
 
(1) An event that involves a release of gas from a pipeline, or of liquefied natural gas, 
liquefied petroleum gas, refrigerant gas, or gas from an LNG facility, and that results in one or 
more of the following consequences: 
(i) A death, or personal injury necessitating in-patient hospitalization; or 
 
(ii) Estimated property damage of $50,000 or more, including loss to the operator and 
others, or both, but excluding cost of gas lost; or 
 
(iii) Unintentional estimated gas loss of three million cubic feet or more; 
 
(2) An event that results in an emergency shutdown of an LNG facility. Activation of an 
emergency shutdown system for reasons other than an actual emergency does not constitute 
an incident. 
 
(3) An event that is significant in the judgment of the operator, even though it did not meet the 
criteria of paragraphs (1) or (2) of this definition. 
 
 
4.1.2 NRC Telephonic Notification Process 
Events on the gas pipeline system meeting the above criteria require immediate telephonic 
reporting to the National Response Center at the earliest practicable moment, but no more than 
one hour after the Company is made aware of the event. 
New York and Maine Companies: Gas Control will make initial incident notifications to the 
NRC per NY O&M Section 9.250, Emergency and SOPs. 
Massachusetts and Connecticut Companies: Gas Dispatch will make initial incident 
notifications. 
Each report shall contain at least the following information: 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 20 of 119


--- Page 349 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
21 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
1) Name of the operator and person making the report and their telephone numbers 
2) The location of the incident 
3) The time of the incident 
4) The number of fatalities and personal injuries, if any. 
5) All other significant facts that are known by the operator that are relevant to the cause of 
the incident or extent of the damages. 
Within 48 hours after the confirmed discovery of an incident, to the extent possible, Avangrid 
must revise or confirm its initial telephonic notice with: 
1) An estimate of the amount of product released 
2) An estimate of the number of fatalities and injuries 
3) All other significant facts that are known by the operator that are relevant to the cause of 
the incident or extent of the damages. 
 
4.1.3 Preparation and Filing of “Written” Incident Reports and Supplemental Reports 
The incident report (Form 7100) shall be prepared by Technical Services and submitted to the 
Department of Transportation at https://portal.phmsa.dot.gov as soon as practicable, but not 
more than 30 days after detection of a reportable incident. 
Supplemental reports shall be submitted as necessary when additional information is obtained 
after the submittal of the initial report. Supplemental reports shall clearly reference the date and 
subject of the initial report. 
4.1.4 Safety-Related Condition Reports 
Safety-Related Condition Reports may be required for conditions on the pipeline system that do 
not meet the criteria in 4.1.1. 
Please refer to the following procedures for assessment of safety-related conditions and 
reporting instructions: 
• 
New York: O&M 9.250 Notifications of Incidents, Outages and Other Reporting, Section 11 
• 
Maine: O&M 3.2.2 Safety Related Condition Reporting Requirements 
• 
MA: O&M 2.3 Safety Related Conditions 
• 
CT: O&M 3.6.2 Safety Related Conditions 
4.2 State/Municipal 
Some events on the gas pipeline system may be reportable to state or municipal agencies. 
Refer to the “Notifications” section in the Company-applicable Part B document for the criteria 
and process by which these notifications are made. 
 
5 Incident Command System  
 
5.1 Structure Overview 
The Companies’ emergency management structure and responsibilities are outlined in this 
section.  This structure is based upon the Incident Command System (ICS) framework 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 21 of 119


--- Page 350 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
22 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
promulgated by in the Department of Homeland Security’s National Incident Management 
System.   
The Companies’ ICS organizational structure establishes common terminology and has been 
modified to accommodate the utilities sector while maintaining commonality to promote liaison 
with public safety agencies and government officials while maintaining an orderly line of 
authority within the ranks. Additionally, the structure helps to promote the ability to more 
efficiently ascertain impacts, set priorities, restore systems and maintain safety.    
The ICS framework is intended to be flexible and expand or contract as a situation warrants. 
Additionally, there is capability to activate an Area Command to coordinate the resources and 
priorities of multiple event or emergency efforts.  As an event escalates, the number of involved 
personnel will also grow and the ICS will expand accordingly to maintain a manageable span of 
control.  
The ICS organization chart is initiated with the activation of the Incident Commander. The 
Incident Commander, consulting with internal leadership, will determine if the activation of the 
general staff and select sections is appropriate given the needs of the situation. Not all positions 
depicted in the organizational charts need be activated for any given situation. 
 
5.1.1 Emergency staffing structure 
There are two major elements to the emergency staff organizational chart: the Incident 
Command Staff and the Area Command Staff. 
Generally, Incident Command Staff (ICS) is activated to respond to a situation in a given 
geography, or within one commodity (i.e. gas or electric). If the situation requires the activation 
of multiple ICS organizations due to disparate commodity, event complexity, or geography (such 
as multiple impacted service areas), the Area Command Staff (ACS) may be activated to 
provide additional oversight and coordination of teams under the unified command principle. 
Should Area Command be activated due to a multiple-area impacting event, each impacted 
area may activate its own Incident Commander, reporting to Area Command. 
Figure 5.1.1 depicts the relationship between the Incident Command Staff and Area Command 
Staff. 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 22 of 119


--- Page 351 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
23 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
Figure 5.1.1 Area Command and Incident Command Relationship 
 
 
5.1.2 Incident Command Staff (ICS) 
The first elements deployed in response to a situation are the Incident Commander (IC) and 
General Staff.  
The IC takes control of the situation from a staffing, tactics and reporting standpoint. Additional 
sections are activated at the direction of the IC, or subsequently the Section Chiefs as required 
by the event needs.  
The figure below depicts the typical ICS structure at full deployment. As described above, not all 
positions are required for every situation.  
In some situations, and geographies, personnel may perform the duties associated with multiple 
roles simultaneously as the demands may not necessitate the activation of multiple persons.

5.1.3 ICS Staff Rosters 
The ICS staff for each operating company are identified in rosters in the “UGEP Internal 
Contacts” document available on the Companies Gas Emergency SharePoint Site. These 
rosters are maintained in a separate document such that assigned personnel and their 
respective contact information can be continuously updated. 
5.1.4 Area Command Structure 
Depending upon the scope of an emergency, the Area Command (depicted in Figure 5.1.1) may 
be activated to coordinate resources and priorities across multiple efforts.  
The Area Commander or Incident Commander has the option of activating whichever positions 
will add value to management of the current event. This supports the key ICS concept of 
maintaining a manageable span of control; typically, with three to seven subordinates to each 
position.

5.2 Multiple/Extended Emergency Guidelines 
If an event or emergency duration exceeds or is expected to exceed a safe duration for 
continuous staffing or multiple events occur concurrently, consideration shall be given for the 
identification of relieving personnel and shift changes to manage fatigue and personnel safety. 
 
5.2.1 Operational Planning Cycle 
Emergencies that are complex, and/or span multiple days may require a formal planning and 
update cadence to keep all involved parties in sync. Incident command has the discretion to 
determine the appropriate means of planning and communicating during an emergency. One 
tool available to incident command is the Organization Planning Cycle, comprising a series of 
prescribed meetings and activity periods. The cycle steps are described below and depicted in 
Figure 5.2.1. The steps are intended as a guideline and it is at the discretion of the Incident 
Commander to implement all, or select, steps in the cycle as needed. 
The Initial Response phase of the planning cycle comprises the following steps: 
1. Incident/Event 
2. Notifications 
3. Initial Response & Assessment 
4. Incident Brief using ICS-201 (Found in Appendix II: ICS Forms) 
5. Initial IC/UC Briefing 
These five steps, in conjunction with the Operational “O” (below), comprise the Planning “P”. 
6. IC/UC Develop/Update Objectives Meeting 
7. Command & General Staff Meeting 
8. Preparing for the Tactics Meeting 
9. Tactics Meeting 
10. Preparing for the Planning Meeting 
11. Planning Meeting 
12. IAP Prep & Approval 
13. Operations Briefing 
14. Execute Plan & Assess Progress 
Steps 6 through 14 (referred to as the “Operational O”) repeat until the Emergency is concluded. 
A new operational period begins after each Operations Briefing. This cycle is depicted below.

The forms contained in Appendix II: ICS Forms, and the additional materials available on the 
Gas Emergency SharePoint Site provide the tools and guidance necessary to plan and execute 
the emergency response and operational planning cycle.

5.2.2 Operational Planning Responsibilities 
The Operations Section develops the tactics and resources necessary to accomplish the 
objectives already established. The ICS 215 Operational Planning Worksheet is used to 
document the tactics and required resources. 
The Planning Section prepares, distributes, and retains records of the Incident Action Plan (IAP) 
packages containing the following elements: 
• 
ICS 202 Incident Objectives Form 
• 
ICS 203 Organization Assignment List 
• 
ICS 204 Crew Assignment List(s) 
• 
ICS 205 Communications Plan 
• 
Job Briefing - Gas Operations 
The IAP package shall be distributed as follows: 
• 
All Operations Section field crew leaders shall be presented the IAP, preferably hardcopy 
distribution should be made. 
• 
A printed copy of the IAP should be posted in the Incident Command Post so personnel 
there may view it. 
• 
A copy of the IAP should be available online for viewing by personnel stationed at offsite 
locations such as the corporate offices. 
 
5.2.3 ICS Working Hours 
When emergencies span multiple days and large numbers of ICS personnel are working, 
consideration shall be given to the scheduling of working hours in the ICS organization. ICS 
Section Chiefs shall consider the appropriate work schedule for incident response including, but 
not limited to, damage assessment and service restoration.  
 
6 Command Centers  
 
6.1 Emergency Command Centers 
For local events and emergencies, channels of communication and field operations are 
coordinated from a local emergency command center. 
 
This emergency command center should be established in a safe location, in reasonable 
proximity to the event or emergency location(s). Consideration should be given to a backup 
location should the primary emergency command center become unsuitable (e.g. due to 
weather, proximity to threat, limitations on space). 
 
6.1.1 Mobile Command Centers 
Emergency Preparedness maintains mobile command vehicles across the Avangrid Networks’ 
service territory for deployment during emergencies. Contact Emergency Preparedness to 
request these resources. 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3)

Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
 
6.2 Emergency Operations Center (EOC) 
For emergencies of wider company impact, the system Emergency Operations Center may be 
activated. The EOC will be activated based on the level and severity of the incident and the 
Companies’ resources necessary to facilitate a prompt, efficient response. 
 
Generally, but not exclusively, the EOC is opened in conjunction with the activation of Area 
Command. 
 
If the primary site is not functional for any reason, a secondary site will be activated 
immediately. 
 
7 Concept of Operations 
 
Although each event or emergency is unique in its situation details, the Companies seek to 
utilize a consistent approach. This section describes the process for responding to any event or 
emergency using the five-phase approach detailed further in Section 7.1. 
It is important to recognize that response to unexpected events are initially handled by first-
response resources geographically opportune, and it often takes time to mobilize additional, or 
specialty-resources to a given locality.  
The first hour or two of any unplanned event requires local resources to respond, quickly assess 
the situation, make-safe (if possible), protect life and property, engage the Companies’ 
emergency response plans, and ensure that any applicable regulatory notifications are made. 
The deployment of the ICS structure will bring more resources and administrative support to 
bear; however, this support generally requires a few hours to stand-up. Once in operation, the 
ICS structure can support the local resources by taking on administrative and support duties.  
The graph below depicts how the “triage” phase of all emergencies is similar in the first one to 
two hours in terms of responsibilities and available resources. For a Level 5 event, those 
resources will remain similar for the duration of the event provided an escalation is not required.

Table 7.1 Event Time and Resources by Classification 
 
 
7.1 Event and Emergency Reponses Phases 
 
7.1.1 Initial Assessment 
For unplanned events, the first step is to process the incoming information, which may be 
inaccurate or incomplete, and to determine what immediate steps should be taken to preserve 
life and property while ensuring the appropriate notifications are made and resources assessed. 
First responders should be gathering and communicating critical information required by Gas 
Control. 
During the initial assessment phase, the applied resources are generally those within the 
closest proximity to the event or emergency, operating under the normal chain of command. 
Should the event or emergency become a classified event at this stage, these resources (and 
potentially others) will be organized under the ICS structure. 
 
7.1.2 Establish Command 
The priority in the response to a classified event or emergency is to establish the responsible 
command. The establishment of command ensures that tactics and objectives can be clearly 
identified and communicated to all parties and that actions are coordinated. 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 29 of 119


--- Page 358 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
30 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
The Incident Commander is responsible for the initiation of the ICS activation, starting with the 
General Staff and Operations Section Chief. Additional sections can be deployed by the Incident 
Commander or the Operations Section Chief as applicable. 
 
7.1.3 Make-safe 
The priority in any gas system event is to control energy to prevent harm to life first, and 
damage to property second. Typically, make-safe may involve the shutting down of gas assets 
to prevent undesirable pressure or flow conditions in an impacted area, or to stop a release of 
gas.  
Curtailment may also be considered to reduce demand on the system. Curtailments will be 
conducted in accordance with the applicable curtailment plans for each operating company. 
Curtailment plans can be found on the Gas Emergency SharePoint Site.  
If system shutdown or isolation cannot be executed immediately, make-safe may involve the 
evacuation of people from the hazardous area until the energy can be controlled. 
Refer to Company O&M manuals for pipeline shut-down procedures. 
 
7.1.4 Condition Assessment 
Pipeline safety is critically dependent on gas system integrity. Following an unplanned event, 
the system should be reviewed for conditions that could impair structural integrity, operability, or 
a recurrence of a potentially unsafe situation. 
The system condition assessment phase allows the Companies to identify the extent of the 
impact of the event or emergency and to plan the following phases. 
Note: Not all events or emergencies may create a gas system integrity hazard. 
 
7.1.5 Restore System Integrity 
For those events which caused, or may have caused, an impact on the integrity or operability of 
the gas system, the restoration of the system’s ability to safely carry gas will be re-established 
through testing, repair and/or replacement as necessary. 
 
7.1.6 Restore Service 
Once system integrity has been validated, the restoration of service to any customers that may 
have been impacted will commence per Company event-specific and O&M procedures. 
 
 
 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 30 of 119


--- Page 359 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
31 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
7.2 Event-Specific Responses 
Refer to Part B for responses to specific types of emergencies such as, but not limited to, gas 
leaks, fire, and natural disasters. 
In addition, the Gas Emergency SharePoint Site contains checklists for specific event types. 
These checklists are intended to aid Incident Command in the response to an event type but 
may not reflect the nuances of any given event; in all cases, the Companies’ shall perform work 
in accordance with approved policies, standards and procedures, augmented by the experience 
and judgement of those responding to the event or emergency. 
 
7.3 Explosion Protocol 
In the event of an explosion involving, or potentially involving the Companies’ gas systems, 
personnel will follow the “Explosion Notification Procedure”, and utilize the Witness Log in 
Appendix IV. 
 
8 Communications During Emergencies 
 
Effective communication is critical for both Company personnel and external parties. Resources 
responding to an incident need to be coordinated and efficient in their response, and external 
parties need to be informed of any risks, critical activities and restoration expectations. 
 
8.1 Internal 
During emergencies, cellular telephone service and company mobile radio are the primary 
communications tools. In the event of a tech-down emergency preventing the use of cellular 
communications, a protocol for effective communication will be developed by the Incident 
Command Staff specific to the event characteristics.  
Rosters and staff assignments will be kept for complex or multi-day emergencies as necessary 
to efficiently coordinate personnel responding to the emergency. 
 
8.2 External 
The public, news media, municipal emergency responders, and government officials shall be 
informed of the emergency and the Companies’ response actions. 
Much of the response work during an emergency will require collaboration with emergency 
management officials, local governments, law enforcement and fire services. The Companies’ 
communication protocols are targeted at providing these entities with a mechanism for receiving 
and providing information during the response process. Additionally, the Companies will 
maintain communications with the media throughout a major event. 
The Division/Region Public Liaison Officer (PLO) and/or Public Information Officer (PIO) are 
responsible for establishing and maintaining communications with public and emergency 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 31 of 119


--- Page 360 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
32 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
management officials and the media in the affected areas during an emergency. These Public 
officials and media contacts are given an unlisted telephone number to reach the 
Division/Region Public Information Officer directly. 
 
8.3 Public and Emergency Management Officials 
Regular updates to the public and public officials on the status of the restoration efforts will be 
performed by the Public Information Officer (PIO). The PIO will engage the resources and 
guidance of the Corporate Communications organization to select the most appropriate media 
and cadence for the messaging. 
For emergencies with an activated Area Command, the PIO duties will be handled by the 
Assistant Area Command (AAC) Public Information Officer.  
The Incident Commander shall ensure all inquiries by public officials are addressed. All contacts 
with public officials will be documented. Depending on the severity and the estimated duration of 
the event, the Companies may conduct conference calls with emergency management officials 
in areas that are severely impacted. 
For state-specific reporting requirements, refer to the applicable state Part B. 
 
8.4 Contact Lists 
Lists of external contacts (such as public and emergency management officials and municipal 
contacts) are maintained on the gas business page of the Companies’ Gas Emergency 
SharePoint Site.  
All contacts will be reviewed and updated as required on an annual basis, or whenever a listed 
contact is known to have changed.

9 Response Resources 
 
The Companies deploy as many crews as it believes are necessary to safely and quickly restore 
service in each affected area.  The number of crews that can be used, in response to a 
particular event, may be limited due to geographic constraints and because of the nature of the 
damage, including closed roads.  
The Companies have a process for supplementing their local workforces with resources from 
other unaffected areas, as well as Mutual Aid (see Section 9.1 below). Several variables, 
including the number and type of crews needed to meet desired restoration timeframes, the time 
to determine the availability of, and to acquire resources, proximity and projected response 
times of resources, are considered in the determination.  
Work hours and schedules are structured to support the maximum restoration effectiveness 
while still fostering safe working conditions. Although the needs of each emergency are unique, 
during the initial period it may be necessary to work around-the clock until the area(s) and 
hazardous situations are made safe and the gas facilities repaired or replaced. After this initial 
period, and until the system is returned to normal operations, continued efforts will minimize 
night-time hours for safety purposes.  
 
9.1 Mutual Aid 
External mutual aid is the utilization of non-Avangrid Networks utility and contractor crews to 
augment the Companies’ workforce.  
Internal mutual aid is the utilization of Avangrid Networks (Company) internal crews and 
contractors from a region outside those affected by the emergency.  
In general, if internal crews are readily available, they will be utilized prior to the engagement of 
external resources. However, the Companies do not hesitate to call for crews from other Utilities 
or Contractors when they believe it is necessary or would contribute to rapid and safe 
restoration.  Also note that as an event progresses, additional resources may be requested and 
deployed due to the dynamic nature of the event and as resource requirements change.  All 
resource movements of this nature are coordinated through Incident Command. 
The Companies maintain external mutual aid agreements with the American Gas Association 
(AGA), the Northeast Gas Association (NGA) and the Southern Gas Association (SGA) to 
secure resources from external parties should company resources be exhausted. 
In the event that external mutual aid is required, the Companies technical subject matter experts 
will work with those entities to ensure that all incoming personnel are appropriately proficient 
and qualified for the work to be performed and that the work will be performed with appropriate 
equipment and to the requisite procedures. 
The logistics (accommodations, meals, vehicles and fueling) will be provided for through the 
Logistics Section of the ICS/Area Command staff. For more details, refer to Appendix I: ICS 
Roles and Responsibilities. 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 33 of 119


--- Page 362 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
34 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
To request mutual aid resources, contact should be made directly with the appropriate trade 
association (AGA, NGA, or SGA). 
Copies of the mutual aid agreements can be found on the Gas Emergency SharePoint Site. 
This process will be initiated when it is determined that the Companies resources are 
inadequate to respond to the emergency. The request(s) will be submitted by Area Command 
staff. 
 
9.2 Mutual Aid from Electric Operators 
During a gas system emergency, it may be necessary to coordinate activities with operators of 
electrical distribution and transmission systems in the area to prevent unintended ignition of gas 
and to ensure that structures are safely electrified prior to the re-introduction of gas service 
during the restoration phase of a gas outage scenario. 
In the majority of Avangrid’s gas operations service territory, Avangrid is also the supplier of 
electricity, and coordination will be achieved through Avangrid’s ICS structure. For those areas 
of Avangrid’s gas service territory that are not served by Avangrid’s electric operations, the 
electric operator may be a dual-commodity utility (serving gas and electric), and thus 
communications channels are already established under Mutual Aid working relationships 
between Avangrid gas and their neighboring dual-commodity peers. 
In any case, Incident Commanders and their staff shall identify the providers of electricity in the 
area(s) affected by a gas system emergency such that coordination of efforts and mutual aid 
may be initiated as appropriate. All requests for communication between Avangrid’s gas 
operations emergency staff and electric operators shall be routed to Area Command, or to 
Avangrid’s Emergency Planning (EP) organization for events and emergencies where Area 
Command is not activated. 
 
9.3 Supplemental Gas 
The deployment of supplemental gas, such as portable compressed gas trailers, may be used 
to maintain continuity of service in the event of a system disruption. Incident Command, in 
consultation with internal leadership, will determine if the use of supplemental gas is appropriate 
based on consideration including, but not limited to: geography, load (demand), weather, 
duration of outage, system pressure, equipment certifications, etc.. 
The use of supplemental gas will be in accordance with approved Company contracts, policies, 
standards and procedures and any applicable state and federal regulations governing its use. 
Regulatory notifications may be required.

10 Post-Incident Testing 
 
49 CFR Part 199 requires that persons whose performance of a covered function5 may have 
contributed to an incident, or cannot be discounted as a contributing factor to accident be tested 
as follows: 
Alcohol: As soon as practicable, but no later than 2 hrs following the incident 
Drugs: As soon as possible, but no later than 32 hours after an accident. 
The Companies will determine if the incident meets the criteria established in Section 4, 
Notifications to determine the necessity of post-incident testing.  
Post-incident testing will be conducted in accordance with the Companies’ policy and specific 
Operating Company procedures as detailed in the Anti-drug and Alcohol Misuse Program 
document (ANHS-SOP-029). 
 
11 Investigation 
 
As provided in paragraph 192.617 of the Minimum Federal Safety Standards, each accident or 
failure shall be investigated for the purpose of determining the causes of the failure and 
minimizing the possibility of a recurrence.  
 
This investigation will occur as soon as possible after the emergency. The investigation will be 
conducted by a team of operations and emergency management personnel, which may include 
an outside consultant.  
 
For those states with state-specific requirements, refer to the “Investigation” section of the 
relevant Part B document. 
 
11.1 Information Capture 
In the event of an accident or failure, all personnel who respond should be aware that they 
should take as many notes and record as many details regarding the situation as is possible.  
Written notes are best but may not be practical during an emergency.  However, as soon as an 
emergency is under control, responding personnel should write down as many details of the 
situation as they can utilizing the appropriate ICS documents, especially actions taken, the time, 
and description of the emergency scene at that time.  These details are very important in the 
analysis of what caused the emergency, evaluating how the Company responded, and 
minimizing the possibility of a recurrence. 
 
 
5 Please refer to your Company’s regulatory organization for definitions of covered function and performs.

11.2 Consideration of Control Room Activities 
In the event of a federally reportable incident and in accordance with federal and state pipeline 
regulation, the actions of Gas Control personnel shall be reviewed to determine if operator 
fatigue may have contributed to the incident. 
In any such case, the applicable Control Room Management policies and procedures will 
govern for the Company and state in which the control room personnel reside.

12 Training 
 
12.1 Operations Personnel 
Field operations leadership (e.g. managers, supervisors) shall facilitate an annual review of the 
plan with operations personnel expected to respond during events or emergencies. 
 
12.2 ICS Personnel 
12.2.1 Initial Training 
Personnel assigned a role in the Companies’ Emergency Plan shall be trained on its contents 
and use as soon as practicable following such assignment. Training may take the form of 
formalized instruction, on-the-job (OJT) instruction, or shadowing of experienced personnel 
during exercises, drills or emergencies. 
Training courses used to support the Unified Gas Emergency Plan may include nationally 
developed, or Company equivalent, training covering use of the Incident Command System 
(ICS) during emergencies. Examples include: 
• 
IS-700 NIMS, an Introduction 
• 
ICS-100 Introduction to the Incident Command System 
• 
ICS-200 ICS for Single Resources and Initial Action Incidents 
• 
ICS-300 Intermediate ICS for Expanding Incidents 
• 
ICS-400 Advanced ICS 
 
12.2.2 Refresher Training 
The Companies will identify the need for refresher training based on observation during, and 
assessment following, event and emergency activation.  
12.2.3 Role-specific Training 
The Companies will identify any role-specific training that may be required for personnel with 
assignment in the emergency plan.  
12.2.4 Changes to plan 
The Companies may identify training as applicable following revisions or updates to the Plan.  
12.2.5 Training Effectiveness 
The effectiveness of training shall be assessed through the execution of drills and/or tabletop 
exercises, and via observation during events and post-event/training reviews in accordance with 
Section 14. 
 
12.3 Other Company personnel 
In addition to those personnel assigned a role in the Plan, additional Company personnel may 
be identified for awareness and familiarity with the Plan for the purposes of increasing 
understanding, coordination and effectiveness during an event or emergency.

12.4 Exercises and Drills 
The Companies will conduct periodic exercises and/or drills with key stakeholders both internal 
and external to the Companies. Exercises and drills will vary in geography and stakeholder 
make-up at the discretion of the Companies. 
For those states with state-specific requirements, refer to “Exercises and Drills” in the applicable 
Part B document. 
 
13 Liaison with External Agencies 
 
During the year, when emergencies are not in progress, the Companies’ outreach personnel 
and other Company representatives contact public officials to discuss emergency procedures, 
response methods and restoration priorities and to exchange contact information.   
Specific activities may vary state to state, but generally include the exchange of emergency 
contact information, emergency plans (such as this document), and education and training 
opportunities. 
For those states with state-specific requirements, refer to the “Liaison with External Agencies” 
section of the relevant Part B document. 
 
14 Continuous Improvement 
 
14.1 Post-event assessment (After-Action Review) 
Once an event or emergency has safely concluded, each affected Division/Region should 
conduct a post-emergency assessment.  For events classified as a Level 4, 3, 2, or 1, the 
assessment shall be documented; Level 5 events, the assessment can be informal. 
The purpose of the assessment is to discuss activities and to identify areas for possible 
improvement. The post-incident assessment generally addresses the following topics: 
• 
What went well? 
• 
What didn’t go well? 
• 
What wasn’t done that should have been done? 
• 
What was done that shouldn’t have been done? 
• 
What impact did specific system configuration(s) or elements have on helping or 
hindering emergency response? 
• 
Was restoration (if applicable) organized and efficient? 
The After-Action Review form is attached in Appendix III: After-Action Review Form 
 
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 38 of 119


--- Page 367 ---
UGEP – Part A 
 
39 
Rev. 1 Effective 10/01/2020 
14.2 Management Review 
Management shall evaluate the Companies’ response to the event or emergency by reviewing 
work crews' efforts, any noteworthy customer reactions or comments. 
The Companies’ management will determine the effectiveness of procedures and training and 
gauge the need for revisions to the Companies’ Plan, operations and maintenance procedures 
and training materials as a result of these post-emergency assessments.   
 
15 Document Administration 
 
15.1 Annual Review 
The Companies shall review the Plan annually and revise the Plan as applicable. Revisions will 
be reviewed with key stakeholders prior to publication. 
Revisions to the Plan or other associated documents will be filed with state agencies as 
applicable by law and/or regulation. 
For those states with state-specific requirements, refer to the “Document Administration” section 
of the relevant Part B document.

1 Introduction 
This document is intended to compliment Section 5 of the UGEP Part A in the listing of Gas ICS 
positions and descriptions. This Appendix is not an exhaustive list of personnel that may be 
activated in an emergency. 
Area Command roles, Electric ICS roles and additional information on Gas ICS roles can be 
located in the Incident Position Guide (IPG) on the StormCenter SharePoint site: 
 
2 Gas ICS Structure Overview 
 
The Incident Commander role leads all aspects of event/emergency management for a 
specified geographic area (Division/Region, or part within a Division/Region) to which he/she is 
assigned. A Deputy Incident Commander may also be named to assist the Incident Commander 
with his/her responsibilities. 
The Command Staff comprises the Public Liaison Officer, Public Information Officer, and 
Safety Officer.   
The General Staff comprises the Operations Section Chief, Planning Section Chief, Logistics 
Section Chief, and Financial/Administrative Section Chief.

3 Incident Commander and Command Staff 
 
3.1 Incident Commander 
The Incident Commander (IC), reporting to the Area Commander (if activated), is responsible for 
all activities and overall management of the regional event/emergency. The command staff and 
general staff directly report to the Incident Commander. 
3.1.1 Public Liaison Officer 
The Public Liaison Officer, reporting to the Incident Commander, ensures communication and 
coordination with other agencies or entities that have also been activated in response to the 
same emergency event.  This may include, but is not limited to, State and Local agencies, 
police, fire, county emergency offices, etc. 
3.1.2 Safety Coordinator 
The Safety Coordinator, reporting to the Incident Commander, is responsible for the 
management and development of personnel safety practices and safety measures and 
procedures.   
3.1.3 Public Information Officer 
The Public Information Officer, reporting to the Incident Commander, is responsible for the 
development and release of timely and accurate information to the media, once approved by the 
Incident Commander.  
 
4 Operations Section 
 
4.1 Operations Section Chief 
The Operations Section Chief, reporting to the Incident Commander, is responsible for 
managing all tactical operations at an incident and focuses on restoration of gas services to 
customers. The Service Branch Director, Trans./ Dist. Branch Director, Pressure Control Branch 
Director, are all direct reports to the Operations Section Chief.  
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 44 of 119


--- Page 373 ---
UGEP Part A - Appendix I 
5 
Rev. 1.0 Effective 10/01/2020 
Figure 4.1 Operations Section Org Chart 
 
4.1.1 Service Branch Director  
The Service Branch Director, reporting to the Operations Section Chief, is responsible for 
maintaining and directing a staff of Area Supervisors and field strike personnel with assistance 
from Operations and/or Dispatch Ops Support clerks to oversee the service department field 
processes.  They prepare initial incident field response work packages, engage in pre-incident 
service protection initiatives, completes and reports executed field activities to Planning Section 
in support of Outage Management activities. The Service Branch Director Provides situational 
awareness to Operations Section Chief to periodic incident briefings. The following roles are 
direct reports to the Service Branch Director: Dispatch Ops Support, Strike Team Leaders.  
4.1.1.1 Dispatch Ops Support 
The Dispatch Ops Support function provides clerical assistance to the Service Branch Director, 
primarily in tracking and displaying information pertaining to, but not limited to, calls received by 
the Customer Relations Center or Call Center, warning tags issued during the incident.  
4.1.2 Pressure Control Branch Director 
The Pressure Control Branch Director, reporting to the Operations Section Chief, maintains and 
directs a staff of Strike Team Leaders and their personnel in overseeing the continued and safe 
operation of gas regulator station equipment. Completes field work activities and reports results 
to the Planning Section. Coordinates with technical specialists and ECC to maintain the 
acceptability of gas distribution system pressures. Ensures adequate gas odorization levels 
within the system. Provides situational awareness to the Operations Section Chief.  
4.1.3 Transmission / Distribution Branch Director 
The Transmission & Distribution Branch Director, reporting to the Operations Section Chief, is 
responsible for maintaining and directing a staff of Strike Team Leaders and their personnel. 
They respond to emergency situations, complete main and service restoration work

5 Planning Section 
 
5.1 Planning Section Chief 
The Planning Section Chief is a member of the General Staff and reports to the Incident 
Commander.  The Planning Section Chief is responsible for all planning activities necessary to 
support restoration activities.  This individual appoints subordinates and expands the Planning 
Section ICS organization, as appropriate, to achieve a manageable span of control and to 
support an effective and efficient restoration effort.  
Figure 5.1 Planning Section Org Chart 
 
 
5.1.1 Situation Unit Leader  
The Situation Unit Leader, reporting to the Planning Section Chief, is responsible for working 
with the Outage Support Team to maintain gas outage statistics by creating reports to 
summarize response statistics and estimate time of restoration. The Outage Support 
Coordinator reports to the Situation Unit Leader.

5.1.1.1 Outage Support Coordinator 
The Outage Support Coordinator, reporting to the Situation Unit Leader, is responsible for 
organizing data that is coming in from the event and relaying it to the Situation Unit Leader. The 
Outage Support Coordinator may lead an Outage Support Team. 
5.1.2 Resource Unit Leader 
The Resource Unit Leader, reporting to the Planning Section Chief, is responsible for 
maintaining the status of all assigned resources activated/deployed during the event/incident. 
Additionally, they are responsible for preparing select portions of the Incident Action Plan.  
5.1.3 Mobilization/ Demobilization Coordinator 
The Mobilization/Demobilization Coordinator, reporting to the Planning Section Chief is 
responsible for overseeing all activities associated with the mobilizing/demobilizing efforts 
including providing information to personnel deploying to the incident or disengaging from the 
effort.  
5.1.4 Document Unit Leader 
The Document Unit Leader, reporting to the Planning Section Chief, is responsible for 
maintaining accurate, up-to-date incident files stored for legal, analytical, and historical 
purposes. The Documentation Unit reports to this position when activated. 
5.1.5 Technical Specialists 
Technical Specialists are ad-hoc roles created when an event/incident requires the specific 
expertise of select personnel not assigned a permanent role in the ICS structure. Technical 
specialists may be engaged from any business unit as the situation requires. Often, Technical 
Specialists are engaged for their blue-sky expertise. They report directly to the Planning Section 
Chief when activated. 
 
6 Logistics Section 
 
6.1 Logistics Section Chief 
The Logistics Section Chief is a member of the General Staff and reports to the Incident 
Commander.  The Logistics Section Chief is responsible for procuring, supporting, and tracking 
all resources necessary to support restoration activities.  This individual appoints subordinates 
and expands the Logistics Section ICS organization, as appropriate, to achieve a manageable 
span of control and to support an effective and efficient event/incident response. The 
Accommodation Unit Leader, Critical Needs Branch Director and the Utility Support Branch 
Director report to the Logistics Section Chief.  
Exhibit __ (EPP-3) 
Page 47 of 119


--- Page 376 ---
UGEP Part A - Appendix I 
8 
Rev. 1.0 Effective 10/01/2020 
 
 
6.1.1 Critical Needs Branch Director: 
The Critical Needs Branch Director, reporting to the Logistics Section Chief, is responsible for 
overseeing contact with Life Support Equipment (LSE) customers and large 
commercial/industrial customers prior to an expected event and regularly throughout an event or 
emergency, to determine the customers’ status and the level of support needed. The Critical 
Facility and Key Account Coordinators report to this role.  
6.1.1.1 Critical Facility Coordinator 
The Critical Facility Coordinator, reporting to the Critical Needs Branch Director, is responsible 
for providing outreach to hospitals, nursing homes, or locations serving as shelters during an 
emergency.  
6.1.1.2 Key Account Coordinator 
The Key Account Coordinator, reporting to the Critical Needs Branch Director, is responsible for 
maintaining contact with large commercial/ industrial accounts.  
6.1.2 Accommodations Unit Leader 
The Accommodation Unit Leader, reporting to the Logistics Section Chief, is responsible for 
arranging food, lodging and other related items for personnel responding to the emergency 
events. 
6.1.3 Utility Support Branch Director 
The Utility Support Branch Director, reporting to the Logistics Section Chief is responsible for 
developing and implementing logistics plans in support of the IAP. This role also supervises 
representatives from Fleet, Facilities, IT and Supplies when activated.
